Let me look at the card.md and check the Rocq installation.

Let me think about the task. This is System F with locally nameless representation, nondeterministic choice. The target theorem: for closed term `t : forall X, X -> X`, for any closed well-formed type U, value v : U, `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v`.

The standard approach: logical relation. Since we need to reduce `tm_tapp t U` to something that, when applied to v, reduces to v. With nondeterminism, the code `t` might use `choice`. Wait — the target says `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v`, i.e., there exists a reduction path from `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v` to `v`. Since `choice t1 t2 --> t1` and `--> t2`, multi-step can pick either branch. Actually, for a term `t : forall X, X -> X`, it could be `choice (Lambda, lambda x:T, x) (Lambda, lambda x:T, x)` etc. Hmm, but also could be `Lambda, lambda x:X, choice x x` — that reduces to x either way.

But wait — can a term of type `forall X. X -> X` reduce to something that diverges? In System F with nondeterministic choice, choice is a term former. `t` could be `choice t1 t2` where both have the polymorphic identity type. Each branch could do anything that's well-typed, e.g., `Lambda, lambda x:X, (choice x x)` — fine. But with choice, could a term of type `forall X. X -> X` diverge? Suppose `t = choice (Lambda, lambda x:X, x) (Lambda, lambda x:X, loop)`? The second can't have type forall X. X -> X unless it diverges... Actually in a simply-typed setting with choice, well-typed terms can still diverge only if... Hmm, System F is strongly normalizing but choice can break that: `t : forall X, X -> X` could be `choice t1 t2` where t1 = identity and t2 = something that loops but is well-typed. E.g., can we write `Omega` at type `forall X. X->X`? With self-application, requires recursive type, not available. So no infinite loops without recursion. Nondeterminism doesn't create loops since choice reduces to a branch. So actually all well-typed terms normalize? System F is strongly normalizing. With choice added, choice t1 t2 → t1/t2 preserves typing, so still strongly normalizing. So `-->*` is strongly normalizing and confluent? Not confluent, but normalizing.

The proof: logical relation. The classic approach:

Define binary logical relation `R : ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop` — but here we might instead use a unary logical relation (realizability) because we only need the result that t applied to v reduces to v. Let's think: We want to show `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v`. The typical free theorem proof: any closed term of type `forall X. X -> X` is observationally equivalent to the identity function. Using logical relations with two different types interpretation (binary relation between two instances).

But since we only have one term, we might use a relational interpretation where the relation at type `X` is "syntactic identity"? The classic "theorem for free" for `forall X. X -> X` says t is extensionally the identity. The proof: define a logical relation `R_ty : ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop` over pairs of terms, where `R_{X} = eq` and `R_{A -> B} = \lambda f g. forall a b. R_A a b -> R_B (f a) (g b)` with closure under reduction. Then parametricity gives `R_{forall X. X->X} t t`, and instantiating X with U, get `forall v w, R_U v w -> R_U (tapp t U applied v) (tapp t U applied w)`. Taking v = w from R_U needs ... but R_U for arbitrary U is not identity.

Alternative approach: unary logical relation "t is observationally identity-like"? Hmm.

Actually the standard approach for proving "identity at type forall X. X->X" for free: Use binary logical relation between the term at type U and itself; then use "identity extension lemma": for any closed U and closed terms v, R_U v v holds? That's not true in general if R_U is defined as, e.g., for U = Bool the relation might be anything. But the identity extension lemma says R_U v v iff v is in some "semantic" interpretation. Hmm, for the free theorem we need: tapp t U v -->* v for any v : U. The logical relation R_U would need to relate v to v for arbitrary v.

The usual proof of this free theorem uses a logical relation where R_X is equality of terms (or "same value"). Then the identity extension lemma: If U is a closed type and v is a closed term of type U, then R_U v v holds... Hmm, actually the identity extension lemma says R_{U} v v iff v ∈ U's semantic interpretation. For U = Bool with v = true, R_Bool true true holds since it's defined recursively. Actually the identity extension lemma in parametricity says: when the relation at type variables is equality, then at any type U, R_U is equality on the semantic domain. But we need it for syntactic terms, and we need "for all v, R_U v v". Hmm, that's the "identity extension" with reflexive relation: any term v : U is related to itself by R_U when the relation at base type is equality of syntax. For U = arrow types this holds only if we have "R_U v v" for all v—which is true if the relation is defined so that at arrows, we require closure under application. But v could be a non-value term; we need to be careful with definition of the logical relation.

Hmm, let's think about what's actually needed.

Goal: `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v` for v a value of type U.

Idea (the "free theorem" proof): Take the binary logical relation between two "worlds": world 1 uses type U, world 2 uses type U as well? Hmm.

Alternative simpler idea specific to this problem: use the unary realizability predicate: define `R_U t` := "t is a closed term of type U (maybe) and t is related". Hmm, we need to capture that `t : forall X. X -> X` behaves as identity. Since `t` is closed and well-typed at that type, and System F is strongly normalizing, `tapp t U -->* ...`?

Let's think about normal forms of closed terms of type `forall X. X -> X`. A closed term of type ∀X. X→X in System F (with choice) — its normal form structure: head normal form is either `Lambda, t'`, or `choice`. Actually since it's strongly normalizing, t reduces to a normal form which is `Lambda, t'` where `t'` is a normal form of type `X -> X` in context X. And then `t'` normal form of type X -> X in context X... By the "canonical forms" lemma, closed value of type X->X is a `tm_abs`. Then inside, the body has type X where X is a free type variable. By a "no confusion"/"free type variable" lemma, any normal form of type X in a context with type variable X and term variable x:X must be x (or choice of x's). So indeed free theorem.

So the concrete approach: prove strong normalization? That's hard in System F with locally nameless in Rocq — reducibility candidates. Hmm, but maybe easier: we can prove the result using logical relations with a suitable relation.

Let's revisit: The standard "theorem for free" proof of `forall X. X -> X` being identity. Use the binary logical relation R with R_X = (fun v w => v = w) (syntactic equality), and at arrows the "reduction-closed" interpretation:

Define `R_U v w` for values? Common definition: for a type U and terms t1, t2:
- R_{X} t1 t2 := t1 = t2  (for type variable X)
- R_{A->B} t1 t2 := forall u1 u2, R_A u1 u2 -> R_B (t1 u1) (t2 u2)  — but this requires the relation to be about terms not values, leading to non-well-foundedness? Actually System F logical relations are defined by recursion on types; at arrow, we quantify over terms related at A. This is a standard (unary/binary) logical relation in a "applicative" style:
  R_{A→B}(t1,t2) := ∀ u1 u2. R_A(u1,u2) → R_B(t1 u1, t2 u2).
This is a "term model" logical relation which is well-founded by type. Then the fundamental lemma works for well-typed terms: if Γ ⊢ t : T, then for related substitutions, R_T(t·σ1, t·σ2). Closed t: R_T(t,t)? We need "identity extension": For all typed terms t1, t2 of type T, R_T(t1,t2) holds when... no. Hmm.

Actually for the free theorem we need to instantiate with t at both worlds: t1 = t2 = t. We get R_{∀X.X→X}(t,t). R_{Ty substitution}(t1 U1, t2 U2) where the relation at type variable is a *relation variable* ρ. Hmm, this gets complicated: the interpretation of a type with free type variables takes an environment of relations.

But here's the simpler path: The type is closed `forall X. X -> X`. Consider the binary relation with R_X = equality of terms (syntactic). Then R_{∀X.X→X}(t,t) = ∀ U1 U2 (closed types?) ∀ (ρ : relation on terms) ... Hmm, actually to instantiate the type variable X with U, we need a relation on U-terms. We'd want at the end: take U1 = U2 = U, and the relation ρ = the "universal relation" (true for all v,w)? If ρ = universal relation, then R_U v v holds for all v – that's what we need. But then the identity extension at base is not equality; that's fine, the fundamental lemma doesn't require it. Let's see:

Define R_Δ(U, ρ) t1 t2 where Δ is a type variable context, ρ maps type variables to relations on terms.This is "binary logical relation with arbitrary relations for type variables". Then for closed type T (well-formed), R_T is a function of the relation assignment for free type vars — here T is closed so no free vars.

Fundamental lemma: if Delta ⊢ t : T and substitutions γ1, γ2 for term vars with R_{T_i}(γ1 x, γ2 x), and ... then R_T(γ1(t), γ2(t)).

Target: t : ∀X. X→X. t is closed, so R_{∀X.X→X}(t,t). Unfolding: for all U1, U2 (or just U), and relation ρ on U-terms... Actually R_{∀X.T}: we need to define what "for all U" means; usually it quantifies over closed types U1, U2 and relations ρ between terms of these types, and then R_{T[X:=U1, U2]}(t1 U1, t2 U2) with ρ interpreting X. Since we apply t to the same U both times: R_{T[X:=U]}(t U, t U) with ρ.

Wait, but t is applied to types: `t U` in the syntax is `tm_tapp t U`. So R_{∀X.T}(t,t) := for all U1 U2 ρ, R_{T[X:=U1,U2],ρ}(t U1, t U2). Hmm, but for binary logical relation, tapp-ing t to U1 and U2 gives terms of types T[X:=U1] and T[X:=U2] respectively, and the relation R_{T with X interpreted by ρ} between such terms makes sense — a standard "logical relation over a type with type-variable environment".

Then we want to conclude `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v`. Take U1 = U2 = U, ρ = universal relation (relate all terms). Then R_{X→X}(tapp t U, tapp t U) with X interpreted by universal relation. Unfold arrow: for all u1 u2 with ρ u1 u2 (i.e., all u1 u2!), R_X(app (t U) u1, app (t U) u2) = ρ(app (t U) u1, app (t U) u2) = true. That gives nothing since ρ = true. Oops.

So we need the relation ρ to be something that gives us the conclusion. The trick for the free theorem: the conclusion is reduction to v. Use ρ := "fun a b => a -->* v /\ b -->* v"? Hmm, we want app (t U) v -->* v. From R_{X→X}(tU, tU): for all u1,u2 related by ρ, R_X(app tU u1, app tU u2) which is ρ(app tU u1, app tU u2). Take u1 = u2 = v with ρ v v holding if we define ρ a b := a -->* v ∧ b -->* v? Then ρ v v holds since v -->* v. Then we get ρ(app tU v, app tU v) = (app tU v -->* v) ∧ (...), giving the goal!

But wait, to instantiate the higher-order part, we need R_{X→X}(tU, tU) with the relation assignment ρ mapping X to ρ. That requires the fundamental lemma for t at type ∀X.X→X, universally quantified over any relation assignment. Yes! So the key point: the logical relation is defined for arbitrary relations at type variables, and the fundamental lemma holds for all relation assignments (that's the parametricity statement). Then we choose the specific relation ρ a b := (a -->* v) ∧ (b -->* v) (or maybe just a -->* v ∧ b -->* v; for the conclusion we can even use ρ a b := a -->* v). Careful: ρ needs to be a "valid" interpretation—no restrictions, any binary relation on terms works. Then:

- R_{∀X.X→X}(t,t) gives ∀ U1 U2 ρ. R_{X→X}[ρ](t U1, t U2).
- Take U1 = U2 = U (both closed wf types), ρ a b := (a -->* v).
- R_{X→X}[ρ](tapp t U, tapp t U) unfolds to ∀ a b, ρ a b -> ρ (app (tapp t U) a) (app (tapp t U) b). Take a = b = v, ρ v v holds by multi_refl. Get ρ (app (tapp t U) v) (app (tapp t U) v) = app (tapp t U) v -->* v. 

So we need to define the logical relation in a way where `R_{∀X.T}(t1,t2)` quantifies over types U1,U2 and relation assignments. But careful: the type T inside the ∀ is in locally nameless form with de Bruijn indices (bound type variable 0). So the interpretation of an open type with de Bruijn indices needs an environment of relations for bound type variables.

Let's set up: `Rty (Δ : list relation) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop` where the environment maps de Bruijn indices? Since types use de Bruijn indices for type variables and free type variables as atoms.

Type interpretation: 
- `Ty_BVar i` under environment ρs (list of relations on terms, index i): I think we can use a list `rs : list (tm -> tm -> Prop)` and index with nth.

- All: R_{∀X.T} t1 t2 := ∀ U1 U2 (ρ : tm -> tm -> Prop) (closed-ness? wf?), R_T (ρ :: rs) (app_ty t1 U1) (app_ty t2 U2)... Hmm, wait. Actually the standard binary logical relation for System F uses "for all types U1 U2 and relation ρ ⊆ U1×U2". But here I think we can get away with quantifying only over *well-formed closed types* U1 U2? Hmm, do we need wf? For the fundamental lemma, when we prove the T_TAbs case: t = tm_tabs t0 : ∀X.T, we need to show R_{∀X.T}(tabs t0, tabs t0). That requires for arbitrary U1, U2, ρ: R_{T[ρ]}(tapp (tabs t0) U1, tapp (tabs t0) U2). Now tapp (tabs t0) U1 --> open_ty t0 U1. The relation should be closed under reduction/equivalence: we need the logical relation to be "closed under beta/step". Standard approach: define the relation on terms and prove it's closed under reduction (backwards) at every type: if t1 -->* t1' and t2 -->* t2', then R(t1,t2) iff R(t1',t2')? Or at least R(t1',t2') → R(t1,t2) ("closed under expansion"/"anti-reduction"). This is needed for the fundamental lemma for terms that reduce (the choice case: R(choice t1 t2, choice t1 t2) needs closure since choice steps).

Hmm. Standard logical relation style for CBV with "term relation closed under evaluation": define R_T(t1,t2) as: exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 ∧ t2 -->* v2 ∧ V_T(v1,v2), where V_T is a value relation defined recursively? Or define R_T directly on terms via the applicative definition and prove anti-reduction lemmas.

Let's think about which structure makes the fundamental lemma provable with least pain in Coq.

The key difficulties:
1. Locally nameless types: opening with a fresh free variable X, and the relation must handle FVar.
2. Nondeterministic choice: need R closed under step in both directions (choice t1 t2 can step to either t1 or t2, so we need: if R t1 and R t2 then R (choice t1 t2)). And in general for the T_Abs/T_TAbs cases, we need R(tabs ...) even though the body has a free type variable etc.
3. The fundamental lemma for T_TApp requires that R_{∀X.T}(t,t) implies R_{T[X:=U]}(tapp t U, tapp t U) — which is exactly the definition of R_All, given the type substitution corresponds to opening. We need a lemma connecting open_ty T U with the relation environment: R_{open_ty T U} t1 t2 = R_T[ρ_U...]. Hmm, since our relation environment maps bound type variables, and the "all" case opens the body with U — but in locally nameless style the "all" case in the fundamental lemma is about `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` for fresh X. Let's think about how to set this up.

Alternative cleaner approach: Define the logical relation on *closed types* only, with a type substitution function. That is, define `Rty (T : ty) (ρ : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop) t1 t2` where T may have free type variables as atoms (no BVars). Then for Ty_All T, we need T to be interpreted by extending ρ with X ↦ ρ. But the body of Ty_All in the syntax is a locally nameless type with de Bruijn index 0. We can convert: the body of ∀ has bound variable 0; to interpret it under a relation for the bound variable, we can use `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` for a fresh X and interpret. That is, we can define:

R_All(∀T, t1, t2) := ∀ X (fresh?), U1 U2, wf U1 -> wf U2 -> ∀ ρ, Rty (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)) (X ↦ ρ) (tapp t1 U1) (tapp t2 U2).

Hmm, but then the relation for `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` with X ↦ ρ interprets the bound variable. Good. But there's subtlety: open_ty T (Ty_FVar X) replaces BVar 0 (and under binders, the indices shift), fine, and X must be fresh w.r.t. T? No — for closed-ish types T, opening any BVar 0 with a free var X is fine as long as X doesn't get captured — FVar X is just a free atom, and if T already had FVar X free (T is the body of a ∀ which came from a closed type, so it can have FVar atoms free from the ambient type context). Since we consider types WF in a context Delta of atoms, we can pick fresh X ∉ Delta. Hmm. This is getting complicated. Let's think about what's actually needed and find the simplest coherent formulation.

Let's consider defining everything with de Bruijn *levels* or with the "open with a named variable" style and a relation environment.

Actually, maybe simpler: interpret types in a "semantic" way indexed by type substitutions. Let me think about the standard Coq formalization of this exact benchmark style (this appears to be from a benchmark suite "System F parametricity"). These tasks typically expect a logical relation:

```
Fixpoint R_ty (T : ty) (env : list (tm -> tm -> Prop)) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop
```

Hmm, but let's think about what's mathematically simplest while remaining provable.

Key insight: we only ever need to relate a term to *itself*, and the relation at type variables is chosen by us at the end. But the fundamental lemma needs full generality for arbitrary relations at type variables because we instantiate X with our chosen ρ. Since the type ∀X.X→X has no other type variables, we only need to handle one relation variable.

Simplification: We can define the logical relation only for types with *no free type variables other than those in the environment* and use de Bruijn indices for bound variables. Perhaps define:

```
Definition Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.

Fixpoint Rty (rs : list Rel) (T : ty) : Rel :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i rs (fun _ _ => False)   (* or some default *)
  | Ty_FVar X => (* free type variable: ??? *)
  ...
```

The problem is Ty_FVar. In the fundamental lemma, for the T_TAbs case we open the body with a fresh FVar X and add X to the type context Delta, and also extend the relation environment. So we need an environment mapping atoms to relations too. Let's use both: `Rty (Delta-env : atom -> Rel) (rs : list Rel) T`.

Wait, but here's a neat trick: Instead of de Bruijn-indexed relation environments, note that `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` turns BVar 0 into FVar X. Then we can interpret opened types using an atom-keyed environment only. I.e., define Rty on types where all bound variables have been opened with atoms. But T_All case: the body T has BVar 0; we open with fresh X and extend the atom environment with X ↦ ρ. Then the type `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` has no BVars at all if T came from a closed type? Let's check: T is the body of Ty_All T, which is a type with BVar 0 bound by the All. It could contain nested Alls with their own BVars. But when T is part of a *closed* type (in the sense of a type derived in the empty type context in the target), all bound variables are eventually opened by the recursion of Rty. Hmm, but at each level we'd define Rty on an arbitrary type possibly with BVars, and recurse into the All case by opening with fresh X. So we need the invariant: types in the relation have BVars that are bound by enclosing Alls *within the type*, not by outside contexts. Since types in the typing judgment may be open (wf_ty Delta T allows FVar atoms from Delta) but have no free BVars, as wf_ty never produces free BVars (all BVar are under Ty_All... wait, actually wf_ty is defined on types where BVars only under All binders indeed: WF_Var requires FVar, WF_Arrow, WF_All with opening. So any wf type has no free BVars. But a type `T` could in principle have free BVar if not wf; in the fundamental lemma we only deal with wf types).

So: define Rty with an atom environment `xi : atom -> Rel` (for FVars) and handle BVar under All by opening with a fresh atom and extending xi. But the body T of Ty_All T is not wf in Delta; it's wf in the "extended" context. Hmm, but we don't need wf for the recursion; we need the opening to be capture-avoiding w.r.t. the atom environment.

Let's define:

```
Definition Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.

Fixpoint Rty (xi : atom -> Rel) (T : ty) : Rel :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => fun _ _ => False   (* shouldn't happen for wf types *)
  | Ty_FVar X => xi X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t1 t2 => forall u1 u2, Rty xi T1 u1 u2 -> Rty xi T2 (tm_app t1 u1) (tm_app t2 u2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun t1 t2 => forall (X : atom) (U1 U2 : ty) (rho : Rel),
      Rty (extend xi X rho) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)) (tm_tapp t1 U1) (tm_tapp t2 U2)
  end.
```

Hmm wait, in the All case, we should only quantify over closed types U1, U2 and wf types, and perhaps a closedness condition to relate with ρ. Actually here's the thing: if we quantify over all types U1 U2 including ones with free BVars, it's fine—it's a bigger quantification, and the fundamental lemma for tabs must prove it for all U1 U2. But the problem: the body `open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)` relates terms of type T1[X] and the terms tapp t1 U1 — but is tapp t1 U1's actual type T1[X:=U1]? For the fundamental lemma we prove the relation for *specific* terms and types; the quantification over arbitrary U1,U2 just needs the relation statement, no type-correctness needed. So it should be fine but we need the fundamental lemma to establish it.

The `Ty_BVar i => False` case: when does this appear? In Rty applied to types with free BVars — which we claim never happens for wf types... but the definition of Rty recurses structurally, so we never evaluate the BVar branch except if the type isn't wf. In the All case we open the body, so internal BVars become FVars; but if T1 has a free BVar (index > 0 relative), open_ty only replaces index 0. Hmm, e.g., body = Ty_BVar 1 (referring to an outer binder) — but for wf types this doesn't occur. Since our relation is only ever applied to types obtained from wf types and their openings, we might be okay. But actually, for the fundamental lemma, we'll need to handle the typing derivation's types which are wf. Let's check: T_Abs has wf_ty Delta T1, and T2 is... hmm, T2 is the codomain type in the typing rule, and we don't get wf_ty for T2 directly? The typing rule T_Abs: `wf_ty Delta T1` and body typed at T2. Does has_type imply wf_ty of the type? Probably yes as a lemma (typing implies wf types). We might need to prove that has_type Delta Gamma t T implies wf_ty Delta T (with Delta containing all free type vars). Hmm, for T_Var we have wf_ty Delta T. Yes the rules include wf_ty for var, abs domain, tapp type argument, and T_TAbs body. But T_App has no wf for T2, T_Choice none, T_Abs's T2 not. So we'd need a lemma: has_type Delta Gamma t T -> wf_ty Delta T, plus environment well-formedness. That's provable by induction.

Alternatively, define the relation to be closed under "all types" without requiring wf, and prove the fundamental lemma syntactically. Let's think about what's needed for the fundamental lemma:

Fundamental: for all Delta Gamma t T, has_type Delta Gamma t T -> for all substitutions σ1 σ2 (for term vars: atom -> tm) related pointwise by... hmm, wait. The term context Gamma has entries (x, T). We need a semantic environment: for each x:T in Gamma, Rty xi T (σ1 x) (σ2 x). Then Rty xi T (σ1(t)) (σ2(t)) — where σ is a substitution of free term variables.

But hold on: the terms in has_type use `open_tm t2 (tm_fvar x)` for binders, i.e., locally nameless with free variables for term variables, and `tm_subst` for substitution. So a substitution σ : atom -> tm applied via tm_subst is the natural semantic action. We need lemmas about substitution/opening and their interaction (standard locally nameless lemmas): opening then substituting = substituting then opening, etc. This could be a lot of work but standard.

Alternatively, we can avoid explicit substitution in the fundamental lemma by doing induction on the typing derivation directly with the target as: Rty xi T t t for closed t (σ = identity). But for the T_Abs case we need to relate an abstraction that binds a variable x: R_{T1→T2}(abs T1 t2, abs T1 t2) = ∀ u1 u2, R_{T1} u1 u2 -> R_{T2}(app (abs T1 t2) u1, app (abs T1 t2) u2). Now app (abs T1 t2) u1 --> open_tm t2 u1. So we need closure of the relation under reduction: R_{T2}(open_tm t2 u1, open_tm t2 u2) from the induction hypothesis that gives (for fresh x) R_{T2}(open_tm t2 (fvar x), open_tm t2 (fvar x)) for all x fresh. Hmm, here the IH is about free variables, and the application uses u1, u2 arbitrary related terms. This is why we need substitution: R_{T2}(t2[x:=u1], t2[x:=u2]).

### Design

**Relations**: `Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop`.

We need:
- An environment for type variables: both free type variables (atoms) and bound (de Bruijn). Maybe easiest: work with de Bruijn indices only for bound variables and an atom environment for free variables. Alternatively, avoid FVar issues by a "closing" construction.

Let me consider defining the logical relation on *types* with de Bruijn indices and a list of relations, and handle free type variables by substitution: Since the typing judgment's type context Delta is a list of atoms (free type variables), the fundamental lemma needs to interpret types that may contain FVar X for X ∈ Delta. So we need a type-variable environment mapping atoms to relations: `xi : atom -> Rel`.

But types with de Bruijn indices: In `wf_ty Delta T`, T can have BVars under All binders. When we hit Ty_All T1, we open T1 with a fresh atom X and extend xi; but the inner type `open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)` may still have BVars bound by inner Alls, handled recursively. And the fresh atom X must not already be in the domain of xi (or if it is, we shadow it; that's fine if we use an environment function with update). Capture avoidance: `open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)` where X is fresh for Delta and for the atoms free in T1... Actually open_ty can capture if T1 contains FVar X already free. But if T1 is the body of a wf type Ty_All T1 where the All is wf in Delta, then the body's free type variables (FVars appearing) are from Delta... hmm, WF_All says for all X not in L, wf_ty (X::Delta) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)). This means that the body opened with a fresh X is wf in X::Delta. In particular, if T1 contained FVar X for some X in Delta, that's fine. When we choose a fresh X (not in Delta), open_ty T1 (FVar X) doesn't capture because X doesn't occur free in T1 (if it did, it would be a free var of the All type which... hmm, actually the type ∀T1 could have free FVar X? No wait: if T1 contains FVar Y free, then Ty_All T1 contains FVar Y free. The WF_All rule quantifies over fresh X not in L; we can pick L to include all free variables. But for the relation, we just need some X not occurring free in T1 and not clashing with xi's meaningful domain. Since atoms are nat and we can always find fresh.)

This is getting complicated. Let me consider an alternative: represent types in the logical relation development after "opening all binders"? No, binders are inherent.

Alternative approach avoiding free type variables in the relation: since our final goal only involves a closed type ∀X.X→X and a closed type U (wf_ty [] U, i.e., closed), maybe we can prove the free theorem for *closed* types only, with the relation interpretation defined on closed types, using a *substitution-based* interpretation for open types? But the fundamental lemma walks through typing derivations with open types.

Hmm, let's think about what the fundamental lemma needs. Perhaps we can prove a *semantic typing* lemma directly for closed instances without a general substitution lemma, by using a logical relation on *open* terms with semantic environments. That's the standard approach and requires the environment machinery.

Let me consider using de Bruijn indices for bound type variables and interpreting types with a list of relations, plus an atom environment for free type variables. Define:

```
Definition Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.

Fixpoint Rty (xi : atom -> Rel) (rs : list Rel) (T : ty) : Rel :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i rs (fun _ _ => False)
  | Ty_FVar X => xi X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t1 t2 =>
       lc_tm_at 0 0 t1 /\ lc_tm_at 0 0 t2 /\
       forall u1 u2, Rty xi rs T1 u1 u2 -> Rty xi rs T2 (tm_app t1 u1) (tm_app t2 u2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun t1 t2 =>
       lc ... /\ forall X U1 U2 rho, (some admissibility) -> Rty (extend_xi xi X rho) (rho :: rs) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)) (tm_tapp t1 U1) (tm_tapp t2 U2)
  end.
```

Issues:
1. `lc_tm_at 0 0` (closed) — but in the fundamental lemma, the terms substituted for term variables are closed only if the context substitution is closed. In the T_Abs case we extend with arbitrary related u1,u2 which are locally closed if R includes lc. Then all substituted terms are closed (since the original t is closed? no—t may have free term variables from Gamma, substituted by closed terms). Actually all free term variables in the source term are from Gamma; the semantic environment maps each to terms related at their types, which include lc. So yes, the sigma images are locally closed. But the source term itself with binders: e.g., sigma(t) is closed if t is closed w.r.t. free vars? t may have bvars? has_type terms are naturally locally closed (all BVars bound by Abs). With locally nameless style + opening in the rules, the terms in typing derivations are... Hmm, the T_Abs rule uses `tm_abs T1 t2` with t2 an arbitrary tm (has BVars). So terms can have BVars (bound by their binder). Substitution of term vars doesn't touch BVars. Opening `open_tm t2 (tm_fvar x)` replaces bound var 0 at depth 0. So terms in derivations are "locally closed at 0" in the sense lc_tm_at 0 0... but wait, `open_tm t2 (fvar x)` replaces BVar 0 by fvar x, and the typing IH applies. Hmm, for the fundamental lemma statement we might require lc anyway and prove typing preserves lc.

2. `Ty_All`: the "for all X" with fresh X. In the fundamental lemma for T_TAbs, we have has_type Delta Gamma (tm_tabs t) (Ty_All T) derived from `forall X ∉ L, has_type (X::Delta) Gamma (open_tm_ty t (FVar X)) (open_ty T (FVar X))`. To show R_{∀T}(tabs t, tabs t), unfold: for all X U1 U2 ρ, R_{open_ty T (FVar X)}[...](tapp (tabs t) U1, tapp (tabs t) U2). We want to use the IH with the type context extended by X: but the IH is about has_type (X::Delta) Gamma (open_tm_ty t (FVar X)) (open_ty T (FVar X)) and its relation under an environment mapping X to ρ. So the environment for free type vars needs to be extended with X ↦ ρ. But the extended type environment in the definition of R_All uses `X` chosen by the quantifier—which needs to be fresh relative to Delta so that it matches the IH's fresh variable. Hmm, but the relation is defined uniformly over all X and all ρ extending xi at X; the IH gives us, for the specific fresh X from the typing rule's hypothesis (forall X, ~In X L -> ...), the statement for all environments xi' extending xi at X. So in the R_All unfolding we need to connect Rty (xi[X↦ρ]) [] (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) with the IH. Since both are literally the same type and environment, that works if the definition quantifies over X and ρ with xi updated at X. But we must ensure the relation at type `open_ty T1 (FVar X)` only depends on xi through X and other atoms; the bound vars rs: the body T1 of Ty_All has BVar 0 bound; after opening with FVar X, T1 may still contain BVars bound by inner Alls, handled when recursing. But T1 might contain BVar j for j>0 if T1 is not "locally closed" under its own All—but for wf types, no. However, in the Ty_All case the recursion `Rty (xi[X↦ρ]) (ρ::rs) (open_ty T1 (FVar X))` — I planned to pass `rho :: rs` for de Bruijn bound vars, but after opening, there are no free BVars from this binder; inner ones are bound by inner Alls and the environment is extended at those Alls. So actually the list rs is unnecessary if we always open with fresh atoms at each All! If T contains BVar 1 free (referring to an outer binder), then open_ty T (FVar X) replaces BVar 0 only; BVar 1 remains free → not wf (since wf_ty never has free BVars). So for wf types, after opening the outer binder, all remaining BVars are bound by inner Alls which we handle recursively by opening at each level. So NO de Bruijn relation environment is needed! So define:

```
Fixpoint Rty (xi : atom -> Rel) (T : ty) : Rel :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => fun _ _ => False
  | Ty_FVar X => xi X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t1 t2 => lc_tm_at 0 0 t1 /\ lc_tm_at 0 0 t2 /\
      forall u1 u2, Rty xi T1 u1 u2 -> Rty xi T2 (tm_app t1 u1) (tm_app t2 u2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun t1 t2 => lc_tm_at 0 0 t1 /\ lc_tm_at 0 0 t2 /\
      forall X U1 U2 (rho : Rel), admissible rho -> Rty (update_xi xi X rho) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)) (tm_tapp t1 U1) (tm_tapp t2 U2)
  end.
```

Hmm, wait. The `Ty_BVar i => False` case: when Rty is applied to a type with free BVars, the result is False, which is fine because it never happens for the actual instantiations; but the definitional equations matter for proofs. With `Ty_BVar` returning False, equations like `Rty xi (open_ty T (FVar X)) ...` are fine.

However, there's a subtlety: in the All case we pass `open_ty T1 (FVar X)`. If T1 itself is `Ty_BVar 0` (i.e., the type ∀X.X), then open_ty gives FVar X, and Rty xi' (FVar X) = xi' X = ρ, good.

**Definitions**

```
Definition Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.
Definition tyenv := atom -> Rel.
```

Interpretation of a type with free type vars in context Delta: Rty xi T, where xi assigns a relation to each atom. For atoms not relevant, arbitrary.

```
Fixpoint Rty (xi : tyenv) (T : ty) : Rel :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => fun _ _ => False
  | Ty_FVar X => xi X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t1 t2 =>
      lc_tm t1 /\ lc_tm t2 /\

      lc_tm t1 /\ lc_tm t2 /\
      (forall (X : atom) (rho : Rel) (U1 U2 : ty),
          admissible rho ->
          Rty (extend_xi xi X rho) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X))
              (tm_tapp t1 U1) (tm_tapp t2 U2))
  end.
```

Hmm, right. In binary parametricity, the relation is indexed by two types (or one type per side). But when we prove the fundamental lemma, we need to match the IH's type with the relation's type. Let's check the fundamental lemma for T_TAbs carefully with this definition:

IH: ∀ xi σ1 σ2, env_rel xi Gamma σ1 σ2 → Rty xi (open_ty T (FVar X)) (open_tm_ty t (FVar X) [σ1]) (open_tm_ty t (FVar X) [σ2]) for all X ∉ L.

Goal: Rty xi (Ty_All T) (tabs t [σ1]) (tabs t [σ2]).

These differ in the type annotations inside the term! But the relation ignores type annotations... EXCEPT: `open_tm_ty (t[σ1]) U1` vs `(open_tm_ty t (FVar X))[σ1]` — the latter has FVar X in the type annotations and in tm_tapp arguments, while the former has U1. Does the relation R_{open_ty T (FVar X)} distinguish them? It treats subterms via structural application; the difference is only in type annotations, which are never inspected... but the relation's *type index* is `open_ty T (FVar X)`, which is fixed. When the relation unfolds at arrow types, it applies terms to arguments and recurses; no term inspection of type annotations. At All types, it applies `tm_tapp` with arbitrary types and recurses with an opened type. The relation's definition never compares the term's type annotation to the type index. And tm_tapp t U vs tm_tapp t U' have different lc conditions if U vs U' differ in lc. Also in the All case, the relation is applied to `tm_tapp t1 U1` and recurses eventually; the type annotations inside t1's subterms differ but the relation ignores them except lc. So we'd need `lc` invariance under type substitution, which holds for wf/lc types.

Let's step back and consider the classic approach in a paper: "Proving the free theorem for forall X. X -> X". The typical Coq formalization of binary logical relations for System F (with de Bruijn) has:

```
Fixpoint LR (Delta : list atom) (T : ty) (rho : env) : tm -> tm -> Prop
```

and the fundamental lemma is proved via "semantic typing" where the type environment Delta is a list of atoms and the relation environment maps atoms to relations that are "compatible with types". The All case quantifies over U1, U2 and a relation between terms at those types; and the relation at `open_ty T (FVar X)` under X↦ρ needs to be connected to the instantiated type. The standard solution uses a lemma: LR is invariant under type substitution: LR Delta (T[X:=U]) ... = LR (Delta with X) T ... with X↦ρ. This is provable by induction on T, but it requires the relation at U to be the right thing, i.e., ρ should be the relation at U! In the All case: for all U1, U2, ρ ⊆ terms, and X↦ρ: LR (T1 opened with FVar X) [xi[X↦ρ]] relates `tapp t1 U1`, `tapp t2 U2`. The idea: terms of type T1[X:=U1]... The relation environment for the opened type maps X to ρ; but ρ is a relation between terms "of type U1 and U2". So the relation needs to know that the terms related by ρ have types U1 and U2. For the final instantiation U1 = U2 = U and ρ = our special relation, we need it to be a relation on U-terms (which it is syntactically: it relates terms, e.g., all terms that reduce to v — but v : U, so only U-terms can reduce to v... well, syntactically any term could reduce to v, fine).

Hmm, I think the cleanest is: **do not index relations by types at all**. Define R_T purely syntactically (as above), and prove the fundamental lemma for arbitrary ρ (with admissibility), where the All case quantifies over all types U1, U2 (with lc). The annotation-insensitivity lemma seems needed. But maybe we can *avoid* it by carefully defining open_tm_ty and showing the exact equality holds.

Alternative standard approach: **prove parametricity via a logical relation over pairs of *differently typed* terms where the source term is the same but we substitute type variables differently**. There's a well-known technique: the "type substitution" in the term is determined by a **type assignment** `θ : atom -> ty` and the relation environment associates each type variable atom X with a relation between terms of types θ1(X) and θ2(X).

OK so the machinery: we need type substitutions θ : atom -> ty and term substitutions σ : atom -> tm, and the relation indexed by two types and a type-variable environment (which for our purposes can be a function atom -> (Rel), with the two types handled by applying θ1, θ2 to the type). Definition Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.
```
Definition tyenv := atom -> Rel.

Fixpoint Rty (xi : tyenv) (T : ty) : Rel :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => fun _ _ => False
  | Ty_FVar X => xi X
  | Ty_Arrow A B => fun t1 t2 => lc t1 /\ lc t2 /\ forall u1 u2, Rty xi A u1 u2 -> Rty xi B (tm_app t1 u1) (tm_app t2 u2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun t1 t2 => lc t1 /\ lc t2 /\ forall X U1 U2 rho, adm rho -> Rty (xi[X↦rho]) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)) (tm_tapp t1 U1) (tm_tapp t2 U2)
  end.
```

**Fundamental lemma**: forall Delta Gamma t T, has_type Delta Gamma t T ->

```
Fixpoint Rty xi T :=
 match T with
 | Ty_BVar i => fun _ _ => False
 | Ty_FVar X => xi X
 | Ty_Arrow A B => fun t1 t2 =>
     lc_tm_at 0 0 t1 /\ lc_tm_at 0 0 t2 /\

       Rty (extend xi X rho) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)) (tm_tapp t1 U) (tm_tapp t2 U)
 end.
```

```
Fixpoint Rty (xi : atom -> Rel) (rs : list Rel) (T : ty) : Rel :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i rs (fun _ _ => False)
  | Ty_FVar X => xi X
  | Ty_Arrow A B => fun t1 t2 => lc /\ lc /\ forall u1 u2, Rty xi rs A u1 u2 -> Rty xi rs B (app t1 u1) (app t2 u2)
  | Ty_All T1 => fun t1 t2 => lc /\ lc /\ forall (rho : Rel) (U1 U2 : ty), adm rho -> lc_ty U1 -> lc_ty U2 ->
       Rty xi (rho :: rs) T1 (tapp t1 U1) (tapp t2 U2)
  end.
```

Let's carefully define the correspondence. Consider a wf type T (in the empty type substitution sense) in context Delta. We interpret it by Rty xi [] T (no enclosing binders). Since the relation's structure doesn't depend on the types except through this recursive interpretation, we can prove a general "agreement" lemma:

**Lemma (open agreement)**: For all T, xi, rs, ρ, X (with X fresh/not in the free FVars of T? and xi' = xi[X↦ρ]):
`Rty (xi[X↦ρ]) rs (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)) ≈ Rty xi (ρ :: rs) T` — but careful about the BVar indices: open_ty T (FVar X) replaces BVar 0 with FVar X but recurses under inner Alls with k incremented: open_ty_rec 1 under an inner All, replacing BVar 1 with FVar X. So for T with free BVars 0 and maybe others? If T is "well-formed relative to a binder", hmm, T may have BVar 1 free? In Rty xi (ρ::rs) T, BVar 0 ↦ ρ and BVar 1 ↦ nth 0 rs. In the opened type, BVar 0 occurrences remain (inner-bound) and BVar 1 becomes FVar X... wait no: open_ty T (FVar X) = open_ty_rec 0 (FVar X) T, which replaces BVar 0 by FVar X, but under an inner Ty_All it replaces BVar 1 by FVar X (since k increments). So the free BVar 0 of T (bound by the outer binder) is replaced everywhere by FVar X, correctly accounting for inner binders. The remaining BVars in open_ty T (FVar X) are strictly inner-bound. So in the opened type, the "free relative to inner binders" BVars are those bound by inner Alls, matching rs. But Rty xi (ρ::rs) T expects BVar 0 to be the outer binder—in open_ty T (FVar X) that occurrence is now FVar X; so the rs list should be rs without ρ. So indeed `Rty xi' rs (open_ty T (FVar X))` corresponds to `Rty xi (ρ::rs) T`, where xi'(X)=ρ, X fresh (not free in T, not shadowing other things).

However, is this literally an equality of relations on terms? The relation definition's recursion is on the type structure, and opening changes BVar 0 to FVar X; the recursive calls: at Ty_Arrow, both recurse on components; at Ty_All, both push ρ_inner; at BVar: LHS on `open_ty T (FVar X)` — wait, open_ty is applied to the whole type at once before Rty recurses, so `Rty xi' rs (open_ty T (FVar X))`: when we recurse into an inner All's body, the body of `open_ty (Ty_All T') (FVar X)` = `Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 (FVar X) T')`, and Rty pushes ρ_inner ∷ rs and recurses on `open_ty_rec 1 (FVar X) T'`. On the RHS, Rty xi (ρ::rs) (Ty_All T') pushes ρ_inner and recurses on T' with rs' = ρ_inner :: ρ :: rs. Hmm, so we need the lemma in a generalized form with a *level*: `Rty (xi[X↦ρ]) rs (open_ty_rec k (FVar X) T) ≈ Rty xi (ρ inserted at position k in rs) T`. Since for the top call k=0 and rs=[], the correspondence is Rty xi' [] (open_ty T (FVar X)) ≈ Rty xi [ρ] T. More generally with a level k and an arbitrary rs, we insert ρ at position k: `Rty xi' rs (open_ty_rec k (FVar X) T) ≈ Rty xi (ins k ρ rs) T`. This should be provable by induction on T, but the term relations are the same and the recursion pushes at both sides consistently. The term side: the terms are identical, and the relations have the same structure. So the lemma's conclusion is an equality of `Rel`s (functions tm -> tm -> Prop), provable by functional extensionality + induction on T. Alternatively, state it as an iff with the same terms.

Hmm, I think the correct typed-relation approach is different: the relation R_{(T1,T2)}[env] should be defined for types T1, T2 that are *two instances of the same open type* under the environment. Let's define:

`Rty (env : list (ty * ty * Rel)) (xi : atom -> (ty * ty * Rel)) (T : ty) : Rel` where T is an open type with free type vars in dom(xi) and bound vars indexed by env. The relation's meaning: Rty [env,xi] T t1 t2 relates t1 (of type T[xi1]) and t2 (of type T[xi2]). The definition:
- BVar i: nth i env gives (U1, U2, ρ) — the relation ρ, and the types U1, U2 are irrelevant for the untyped relation but recorded.
- FVar X: xi X's relation.
- Arrow: recursion as usual.
- All T1: push (U1, U2, ρ) onto env; recurse on T1 with the terms tapp t1 U1, tapp t2 U2.

So the typed environment approach works with the fundamental lemma carrying θ1, θ2. Let's re-examine the T_TApp case under this scheme:

Let's restrict: For our uses, we need the open lemma only in these scenarios:
1. Fundamental lemma T_TAbs: connect Rty env xi (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) with Rty (ρ::env) xi T1, where T1's only free BVar is 0 (relative to this binder) — i.e., T1 is the body of a wf Ty_All and has no other free BVars. Then no index-shifting issue.

In both cases, T is the body of an All and has no free BVars other than 0 (relative to its binder). More precisely, define the notion: `lc_ty_at 1 T`? A type's BVars: lc_ty_at k T means all free BVars are < k. For T = body of an All, T can have BVar 0 free (if the All binds it) — but T is "closed relative to one binder", i.e., lc_ty_at 1 T. And open_ty T U has no free BVars (lc_ty_at 0) if U is lc... So the opening lemma:

**Lemma (Rty open at 0)**: If `lc_ty_at 1 T` (so T's free BVars ⊆ {0}) — hmm, actually lc_ty_at 1 T means free BVars < 1, so only BVar 0. Then:
`Rty env xi (open_ty T U) ≈ Rty (Rty env xi U :: env) xi T` for any U with no free BVars? Let's check by induction on T:
- T = BVar 0: LHS = Rty env xi U; RHS = nth 0 (R::env) = R = Rty env xi U ✓ (definitionally, since open_ty (BVar 0) U = U).
- T = BVar i with i ≠ 0 impossible under lc_ty_at 1 (i would be ≥1).
- T = FVar X: LHS = xi X = RHS ✓.
- T = Arrow T1 T2: LHS = Rty env xi (open_ty T1 U → open_ty T2 U) = arrow relation of the two; RHS = Rty (R::env) xi (T1→T2) = arrow with components Rty (R::env) xi Ti. By IH (lc_ty_at 1 Ti holds), each ≈ Rty env xi (open_ty Ti U). The arrow relation construction: fun t1 t2 => lc ∧ lc ∧ ∀u1 u2, Rel_A u1 u2 -> Rel_B (app..). If Rel_A ≈ Rel_A' and Rel_B ≈ Rel_B' (as relations), then the arrow relations are equivalent? The arrow relation only uses Rel_A in the antecedent and Rel_B in the conclusion; if they're equivalent relations, yes: for all u1 u2, Rel_A u1 u2 ↔ Rel_A' u1 u2, and Rel_B x y ↔ Rel_B' x y. So the arrow relations are logically equivalent (as functions, with extensionality). To avoid extensionality pain, we can state the lemma as an iff on term pairs:

`Rty env xi (open_ty T U) a b <-> Rty (Rty env xi U :: env) xi T a b` for all a b, under lc conditions.

Then induction works: Arrow case: Rty env xi (open A B) a b ↔ (lc a ∧ lc b ∧ ∀u1 u2, Rty env xi (open A) u1 u2 → Rty env xi (open B) (app a u1) (app b u2)). RHS similarly. By IH: Rty env xi (open A) u1 u2 ↔ Rty (R::env) xi A u1 u2 and similarly for B. So the iff holds. ✓ (No function extensionality needed since we state pointwise.)

But hold on: in the All case of T, we have T = Ty_All T1 where lc_ty_at 1 T means lc_ty_at 2 T1. **Env agreement lemma**: If `lc_ty_at d T` and env1, env2 agree on the first d entries (i.e., forall i < d, nth i env1 = nth i env2), then Rty env1 xi T ≈ Rty env2 xi T (pointwise iff). Hmm, but the relation has sub-relations; prove by induction on T:
- BVar i: lc_ty_at d (BVar i) gives i < d; nth i env1 = nth i env2 ✓.
- FVar: same xi ✓.
- Arrow: components have lc_ty_at d; IH ✓.
- All T1: lc_ty_at d (All T1) = lc_ty_at (d+1) T1. Both sides push ρ (the same quantified ρ), so env1' = ρ::env1, env2' = ρ::env2 agree on the first d+1 ✓; IH ✓.

Great. So Rty env xi U is invariant under env when U has no free BVars (d=0). ✓.

Now back to the open lemma with level: we can state it as: for all T with lc_ty_at (k+1) T? Let's define the insertion more carefully. The general open lemma:

So the **open lemma**: for all U with lc_ty_at 0 U, all k, T with lc_ty_at (k+1) T, all env, xi, a, b:
`Rty env xi (open_ty_rec k U T) a b <-> Rty (ins_at k (Rty env xi U) env) xi T a b`.
Wait, careful: in the All case, the inserted relation is `Rty (ρ'::env) xi U`, and I claimed it equals `Rty env xi U` by env-invariance since U has no free BVars. Let me redefine cleanly with the de Bruijn environments and the "exists value" style:

```
Fixpoint Vty (env : list Rel) (xi : atom -> Rel) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i env (fun _ _ => False)
  | Ty_FVar X => xi X
  | Ty_Arrow A B => fun v1 v2 =>
      (forall w1 w2, Vty env xi A w1 w2 -> Rty env xi B (tm_app v1 w1) (tm_app v2 w2))
  | Ty_All T1 => fun v1 v2 =>
      forall (rho : Rel) (U1 U2 : ty),
        adm rho -> lc_ty_at 0 U1 -> lc_ty_at 0 U2 ->
        Rty (rho :: env) xi T1 (tm_tapp v1 U1) (tm_tapp v2 U2)
  end
with Rty (env : list Rel) (xi : atom -> Rel) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  fun t1 t2 => lc_tm_at 0 0 t1 /\ lc_tm_at 0 0 t2 /\ value t1 /\ value t2 /\
    match T with
    | Ty_BVar i => nth i env (fun _ _ => False) t1 t2
    | Ty_FVar X => xi X t1 t2
    ...
```

Hmm, mutual recursion mixing "term relation = exists value" and "value relation" gets messy because V_arrow's R_B application, etc. Let's simplify: define Rty as an inductive-ish "exists reduction to related values":

```
Definition Rty env xi T t1 t2 :=
  exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 /\ t2 -->* v2 /\ Vty env xi T v1 v2.
```

and Vty by recursion on T. But then V_arrow refers to Rty at B, and V_all refers to Rty at T1. Rty refers to Vty at T. This is a nested recursive definition: Vty T calls Rty at subterms of T (for arrow: B is a subterm; for all: T1 is a subterm), and Rty T calls Vty T. This is definable as a Fixpoint on T with Rty defined inside as a let? In Coq, we can define:

```
Fixpoint Vty env xi T : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | BVar i => nth i env FalseRel
  | FVar X => xi X
  | Arrow A B => fun v1 v2 => forall w1 w2, Vty env xi A w1 w2 -> 
       exists u1 u2, tm_app v1 w1 -->* u1 /\ tm_app v2 w2 -->* u2 /\ Vty env xi B u1 u2
  | All T1 => fun v1 v2 => forall rho U1 U2, adm rho -> lc_ty U1 -> lc_ty U2 ->
       exists u1 u2, tm_tapp v1 U1 -->* u1 /\ tm_tapp v2 U2 -->* u2 /\ Vty (rho::env) xi T1 u1 u2
  end.
```

That directly inlines Rty as "exists values reachable + Vty". Then everything is a single Fixpoint. The term relation isn't a separate definition, but we can define `Rty env xi T t1 t2 := exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 /\ t2 -->* v2 /\ Vty env xi T v1 v2` after the fact. This is much cleaner! And V_arrow's requirement is: for all value arguments w1,w2 related by V_A, the applications reduce to values related by V_B. This is exactly a semantic value relation in "may-converge" style.

- xi maps atoms to admissible relations.
- σ1,σ2 related pointwise at Gamma's types: for x:Ti in Gamma, Rty env xi Ti (σ1 x) (σ2 x). (Note: no θ applied to σ images.)
then Rty env xi T ((t[θ1])[σ1]) ((t[θ2])[σ2]).

Let me now also re-examine the *definition* of Vty to make sure the recursion is structurally decreasing:
- Arrow A B: refers to Vty env xi A and Vty env xi B (subterms) ✓, and Rty env xi B (subterm) ✓.
- All T1: refers to Rty (ρ::env) xi T1 (subterm) ✓.
So a nested fixpoint Vty with Rty defined in terms of it is fine as a mutual recursion where Rty T is defined using Vty T at the same type — that's not structurally decreasing if we make them mutual! Better: define Vty as a Fixpoint where the arrow/all clauses inline the existential for Rty at the subterm type:

```
Fixpoint Vty env xi T : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | BVar i => fun v1 v2 => value v1 /\ value v2 /\ nth i env (fun _ _ => False) v1 v2
  | FVar X => fun v1 v2 => value v1 /\ value v2 /\ xi X v1 v2
  | Arrow A B => fun v1 v2 => value v1 /\ value v2 /\
       forall w1 w2, Vty env xi A w1 w2 ->
         exists u1 u2, tm_app v1 w1 -->* u1 /\ tm_app v2 w2 -->* u2 /\ Vty env xi B u1 u2
  | All T1 => fun v1 v2 => value v1 /\ value v2 /\
       forall rho U1 U2, lc_ty_at 0 U1 -> lc_ty_at 0 U2 ->
         exists u1 u2, tm_tapp v1 U1 -->* u1 /\ tm_tapp v2 U2 -->* u2 /\ Vty (rho :: env) xi T1 u1 u2
  end.

Definition Rty env xi T t1 t2 :=
  lc_tm_at 0 0 t1 /\ lc_tm_at 0 0 t2 /\
  exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 /\ t2 -->* v2 /\ Vty env xi T v1 v2.
```

Good: Vty recurses structurally on T; each recursive call is on a strict subterm ✓. Rty is a plain definition. The clauses inline the "exists reachable value" for the applications. This is a clean definition.

Now, wait: in the Arrow clause, should the argument be `Vty env xi A w1 w2` (values) or `Rty` (terms)? Values, since we need to beta-reduce. ✓. Hmm, Rty includes lc_tm_at 0 0 w1. Do we know lc w1 from Vty env xi T1 w1 w2? Now, the fundamental lemma proof. Let me write it as:

```
Lemma fundamental : forall Delta Gamma t T,
  has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall env xi theta1 theta2 sigma1 sigma2,
    (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (theta1 X)) ->
    (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (theta2 X)) ->
    (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (sigma1 x)) ->
    (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (sigma2 x)) ->
    (forall x Ti, lookup_context x Gamma = Some Ti -> Rty env xi Ti (sigma1 x) (sigma2 x)) ->
    Rty env xi T (tm_subst_env sigma1 (tm_ty_subst_env theta1 t))
                 (tm_subst_env sigma2 (tm_ty_subst_env theta2 t)).
```

Let me now enumerate the syntactic lemmas needed:

**Substitution machinery:**
1. `tm_subst_env` (substitute all free term vars).
2. `tm_ty_subst_env` (substitute all free type vars).
3. `ty_subst_env`.
4. Commutation: `tm_subst_env σ (tm_ty_subst_env θ t) = tm_ty_subst_env θ (tm_subst_env σ t)` (both structural). Might not even need to state if we define a combined substitution? We'll see.

   `tm_ty_subst_env θ' (open_tm_ty_rec k U t) = open_tm_ty_rec k (ty_subst_env θ' U) (tm_ty_subst_env θ' t)`? Now, the **target theorem**:
```
Theorem polymorphic_identity_theorem_for_free : forall t,
  has_type [] empty t (Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 0) (Ty_BVar 0))) ->
  forall U v, wf_ty [] U -> value v -> has_type [] empty v U ->
  tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v.
```
Apply the fundamental lemma with Delta = [], Gamma = [], env = [], xi := fun X => ρ (any function; only one type var? xi is total, so set xi X := ρ for all X), θ1 = θ2 = identity (fun X => Ty_FVar X), σ1 = σ2 = identity (fun x => tm_fvar x), T = ∀X.X→X.
Hypotheses: θ lc ✓ (fvar); σ lc ✓ (fvar is lc); env condition vacuous ✓.
Conclusion: Rty [] xi (Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (BVar 0)(BVar 0))) (t) (t) — since tm_subst_env id (tm_ty_subst_env id t) = t? Hmm, need lemmas: tm_ty_subst_env (fun X => FVar X) t = t and tm_subst_env (fun x => fvar x) t = t (identity substitution) ✓ provable by induction.
Unfold Rty: lc ∧ lc ∧ exists v1 v2, t -->* v1 ∧ t -->* v2 ∧ Vty [] xi (∀(BVar0→BVar0)) v1 v2.
V_∀: forall ρ' U1 U2, lc U1 -> lc U2 -> exists u1 u2, tapp v1 U1 -->* u1 ∧ tapp v2 U2 -->* u2 ∧ Vty (ρ'::[]) xi (BVar0→BVar0)(u1,u2).
Choose ρ' := ρ (our relation), U1 = U2 = U (lc from wf_ty [] U → lc) ✓.
V_arrow: value u1 ∧ value u2 ∧ forall w1 w2, V_{BVar0}(w1,w2) -> exists s1 s2, app u1 w1 -->* s1 ∧ app u2 w2 -->* s2 ∧ V_{BVar0}(s1,s2).
V_{BVar0}(w1,w2) = value w1 ∧ value w2 ∧ ρ w1 w2 where ρ a b := (a -->* v) ∧ (b -->* v).
Take w1 = w2 = v: value v ✓, ρ v v = (v -->* v) ∧ (v -->* v) ✓.
Get s1, s2 with app u1 v -->* s1 ∧ V_{BVar0}(s1,s2) → s1 -->* v... wait, V_{BVar0}(s1,s2) = value s1 ∧ value s2 ∧ ρ s1 s2 = ... ∧ s1 -->* v ∧ s2 -->* v. So app u1 v -->* s1 -->* v ✓.
Compose: tapp t U -->* u1 (from the V_∀ existential with v1), and app (tapp t U) v -->* app u1 v (congruence: app_l needs lc v ✓), then -->* s1 -->* v ✓.

Wait, the goal is app (tapp t U) v -->* v. We have tapp t U -->* u1 and app u1 v -->* s1 -->* v. So app (tapp t U) v -->* app u1 v ✓ (multi_app_l with lc v) then -->* v ✓.

Hmm, one thing: in V_∀'s quantifier we choose U1 = U2 = U, and the values u1, u2 come from the existential. V_arrow(u1, u2) gives forall w1 w2... so u1, u2 need not be abs! The tapp of a tabs reduces via ST_TAppTabs; the resulting u1 is the open body, which should be an abs for the app to reduce. V_arrow(u1,u2)'s forall clause requires app u1 v -->* s1 where V_BVar0(s1,s2) requires s1, s2 values with s1 -->* v. If u1 is not an abs but e.g. an fvar or tabs, then app u1 v is stuck and no such s1 exists, making V_arrow false—but the fundamental lemma guarantees V_arrow for well-typed terms, so u1 must be an abs. We just use the clause; no need to know its structure. ✓.

So the final theorem follows.

Let me now write the Vty open lemmas:

**Lemma Vty_open_FVar** (level-generalized):
```
forall k T env xi X rho,
  X ∉ ty_fv T ->
  lc_ty_at (k+1) T ->
  forall a b,
    Vty env (fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then rho else xi Y) (open_ty_rec k (Ty_FVar X) T) a b <->
    Vty (insert_at k rho env) xi T a b.
```

**Lemma Vty_open_ty** (level-generalized):
```

  lc_ty_at 0 U ->

```

- i = k: LHS Vty env xi U; RHS nth k (insert k R env) = R = Vty env xi U ✓.

Now, let's think about how to define the term substitution environment and prove its lemmas in Coq. Since the terms are locally nameless with free variables as atoms, `tm_subst_env σ t` replaces free fvars by σ. The standard definitions:

```
Fixpoint tm_subst_env (σ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => σ x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (tm_subst_env σ t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_subst_env σ t1) (tm_subst_env σ t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_subst_env σ t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_subst_env σ t1) T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_subst_env σ t1) (tm_subst_env σ t2)
  end.
```

And type substitution env:

```
Fixpoint ty_subst_env (θ : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => θ X
  | Ty_Arrow A B => Ty_Arrow (ty_subst_env θ A) (ty_subst_env θ B)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_subst_env θ T1)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_ty_subst_env (θ : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => tm_fvar x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (ty_subst_env θ T) (tm_ty_subst_env θ t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_ty_subst_env θ t1) (tm_ty_subst_env θ t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_ty_subst_env θ t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_ty_subst_env θ t1) (ty_subst_env θ T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_ty_subst_env θ t1) (tm_ty_subst_env θ t2)
  end.
```

Important: ty_subst_env must not substitute bound variables (BVar) — it doesn't ✓. But NOTE: the existing `ty_subst` substitutes a free FVar X with U, no capture issues since types have no binders over FVars. Our θ replaces FVars by arbitrary types; since FVars are "free" and there's no capture (no FVar binders), this is capture-avoiding ✓.

Hmm, wait: tm_ty_subst_env under tm_tabs: the body is open in the bound type variable? In locally nameless, tm_tabs binds a *type* variable for the types inside; the body's types use BVar indices. ty_subst_env leaves BVars alone ✓. Free FVars are global ✓. Good.

Lemmas:
1. `tm_ty_subst_env_ext`: if θ1 and θ2 agree on fv_ty t, then equal. Needed? Maybe.
2. `tm_subst_env_ext`: if σ1, σ2 agree on fv_tm t, equal.
3. `ty_subst_env` composition: ty_subst_env θ' (open_ty_rec k U T) = open_ty_rec k (ty_subst_env θ' U) (ty_subst_env θ' T).
4. `tm_ty_subst_env_open_ty_rec`: tm_ty_subst_env θ' (open_tm_ty_rec k U t) = open_tm_ty_rec k (ty_subst_env θ' U) (tm_ty_subst_env θ' t).
5. `tm_subst_env_open_tm_rec`: tm_subst_env σ (open_tm_rec k v t) = open_tm_rec k (tm_subst_env σ v) (tm_subst_env σ t).
6. `tm_subst_env_tm_ty_subst_env` commute.
7. `tm_subst_env_open_tm_ty_rec` commute.
8. lc preservation lemmas.
9. `lc_has_type` and `wf_has_type`.

11. Value inversion: value -> lc.
12. Values don't step / multi from value is refl.
13. Multi congruence lemmas.

General wf_ty_subst_env:
```
Lemma wf_ty_subst_env : forall Delta' Delta T theta,
  wf_ty Delta' T ->
  (forall X, In X Delta' -> wf_ty Delta (theta X)) ->
  wf_ty Delta (ty_subst_env theta T).
```
Proof by induction on the wf_ty derivation (or on T with inversion).
- WF_Var X Hin: apply H0 ✓.
- WF_Arrow: IH ✓.
- WF_All T L Hbody: Need wf_ty Delta (Ty_All (ty_subst_env theta T)) via WF_All with some L'. For each Y ∉ L', need wf_ty (Y::Delta) (open_ty (ty_subst_env theta T) (FVar Y)).
  Choose Y fresh: Y ∉ L ∪ Delta ∪ (some finite set of FVars of T and theta...). Hmm, we need Y ∉ fv(T) maybe for the commutation with θ[Y? Let's do the standard approach: from Hbody at X = Y (Y ∉ L): wf_ty (Y::Delta') (open_ty T (FVar Y)). Apply the IH with Delta' := Y::Delta', Delta := Y::Delta, theta' := theta[Y ↦ FVar Y]. ```

```
Fixpoint insert_at (k : nat) (r : Rel) (env : list Rel) : list Rel :=
```

```
  | [] => [r]
```

```coq
Lemma nth_insert_at_eq : forall env k r, k <= length env -> nth k (insert_at k r env) (fun _ _ => False) = r.
```

```coq
Fixpoint ty_subst_env (theta : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => theta X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_subst_env theta T1) (ty_subst_env theta T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_subst_env theta T1)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_ty_subst_env (theta : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => tm_fvar x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (ty_subst_env theta T) (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1) (tm_ty_subst_env theta t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1) (ty_subst_env theta T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1) (tm_ty_subst_env theta t2)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_subst_env (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (tm_subst_env sigma t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_subst_env sigma t1) (tm_subst_env sigma t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_subst_env sigma t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_subst_env sigma t1) T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_subst_env sigma t1) (tm_subst_env sigma t2)
  end.
```

```coq
(* ===================== Auxiliary development ===================== *)

Definition Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.

Fixpoint max_atom_list (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_atom_list l')
  end.

Definition fresh_atom (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_atom_list l).

Lemma max_atom_list_ge : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_atom_list l.
Proof.
  intros l x H. induction l as [| a l' IH]; simpl in *.
  - inversion H.
  - destruct H as [-> | H].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; assumption | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_atom_notin : forall l, ~ In (fresh_atom l) l.
Proof.
  intros l H. unfold fresh_atom in H.
  apply max_atom_list_ge in H. lia.
Qed.
```

```coq
Definition bot_rel : Rel := fun _ _ => False.

Fixpoint insert_at (k : nat) (r : Rel) (env : list Rel) : list Rel :=
  match env with
  | [] => [r]
  | e :: env' => match k with 0 => r :: e :: env' | S k' => e :: insert_at k' r env' end

Lemma nth_insert_at_lt : forall env k r i,
  k <= length env -> i < k ->
  nth i (insert_at k r env) bot_rel = nth i env bot_rel.
Proof.
  induction env as [| e env' IH]; intros k r i Hk Hi.
  - simpl in Hk. assert (k = 0) by lia. subst. lia.
  - destruct k as [| k'].
    + lia.
    + simpl. destruct i as [| i'].
      * reflexivity.
      * apply IH; simpl in Hk; lia.
Qed.
```

Let me carefully write Coq code.

```coq
(* ============================================================ *)
(* Auxiliary infrastructure for the logical relation.            *)
(* ============================================================ *)

Definition Rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.

Definition bot_rel : Rel := fun _ _ => False.

Fixpoint max_atom_list (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_atom_list l')
  end.

Definition fresh_atom (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_atom_list l).

Lemma max_atom_list_ge : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_atom_list l.
Proof.
  intros l x H. induction l as [| a l' IH]; simpl in *.
  - inversion H.
  - destruct H as [-> | H].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; assumption | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_atom_notin : forall l, ~ In (fresh_atom l) l.
Proof.
  intros l H. unfold fresh_atom in H.
  pose proof (max_atom_list_ge l _ H). lia.
Qed.

Fixpoint insert_at (k : nat) (r : Rel) (env : list Rel) : list Rel :=
  match env with
  | [] => [r]
  | e :: env' =>
      match k with
      | 0 => r :: e :: env'
      | S k' => e :: insert_at k' r env'
      end
  end.

Lemma nth_insert_at_lt : forall env k r i,
  k <= length env -> i < k ->
  nth i (insert_at k r env) bot_rel = nth i env bot_rel.
Proof.
  induction env as [| e env' IH]; intros k r i Hk Hi.
  - simpl in Hk. assert (k = 0) by lia. subst. lia.
  - destruct k as [| k'].
    + lia.
    + simpl. destruct i as [| i'].
      * reflexivity.
      * apply IH; simpl in Hk; lia.
Qed.

Lemma nth_insert_at_eq : forall env k r,
  k <= length env -> nth k (insert_at k r env) bot_rel = r.
Proof.
  induction env as [| e env' IH]; intros k r Hk.
  - simpl in Hk. assert (k = 0) by lia. subst. reflexivity.
  - destruct k as [| k']; simpl.
    + reflexivity.
    + apply IH. simpl in Hk. lia.
Qed.
```

Now fv:

```coq
Fixpoint ty_fv (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar _ => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => ty_fv T1 ++ ty_fv T2
  | Ty_All T1 => ty_fv T1
  end.

Fixpoint tm_fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs _ t1 => tm_fv t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_fv t1 ++ tm_fv t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_fv t1
  | tm_tapp t1 _ => tm_fv t1
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_fv t1 ++ tm_fv t2
  end.
```

Substitutions.

```coq
Fixpoint ty_subst_env (theta : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => theta X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_subst_env theta T1) (ty_subst_env theta T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_subst_env theta T1)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_ty_subst_env (theta : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => tm_fvar x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (ty_subst_env theta T) (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1) (tm_ty_subst_env theta t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1) (ty_subst_env theta T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_ty_subst_env theta t1) (tm_ty_subst_env theta t2)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_subst_env (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (tm_subst_env sigma t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_subst_env sigma t1) (tm_subst_env sigma t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_subst_env sigma t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_subst_env sigma t1) T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_subst_env sigma t1) (tm_subst_env sigma t2)
  end.
```

Lemmas:

```coq
Lemma tm_ty_subst_env_open_tm : forall theta d v t,
  tm_ty_subst_env theta (open_tm_rec d v t) =
  open_tm_rec d (tm_ty_subst_env theta v) (tm_ty_subst_env theta t).
Proof.
  intros theta d v t. revert d. induction t; intros d; simpl; auto.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb d n); reflexivity.
  - rewrite IHt1, IHt2. reflexivity.
  - rewrite IHt1, IHt2. reflexivity.
Qed.
```
Wait, open_tm_rec is defined with recursion over t; in the tm_bvar case, `open_tm_rec d v (tm_bvar n) = if Nat.eqb d n then v else tm_bvar n`. RHS: open_tm_rec d (tm_ty_subst_env theta v) (tm_ty_subst_env theta (tm_bvar n)) = open_tm_rec d (tm_ty_subst_env theta v) (tm_bvar n) = if eqb d n then tm_ty_subst_env theta v else tm_bvar n. LHS: tm_ty_subst_env theta (if eqb d n then v else tm_bvar n). If eqb d n true: LHS = tm_ty_subst_env theta v ✓. Else: LHS = tm_bvar n ✓. So destruct eqb ✓.

tm_fvar: open gives tm_fvar x; tm_ty_subst_env gives tm_fvar x ✓; RHS similarly (open of tm_fvar? open_tm_rec d _ (tm_fvar x) = tm_fvar x) ✓.

tm_abs: open = tm_abs T (open_tm_rec (S d) v t1); tm_ty_subst_env theta gives tm_abs (ty_subst_env theta T) (tm_ty_subst_env theta (open_tm_rec (S d) v t1)) = by IH with d:=S d: tm_abs (θ T) (open_tm_rec (S d) (θ v) (θ t1)). RHS: open_tm_rec d (θ v) (tm_ty_subst_env theta (tm_abs T t1)) = open_tm_rec d (θ v) (tm_abs (θ T) (θ t1)) = tm_abs (θ T) (open_tm_rec (S d) (θ v) (θ t1)) ✓.

tm_tabs: open = tm_tabs (open_tm_rec d v t1) (note: open_tm_rec doesn't increment d for tm_tabs according to the definition: `| tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (open_tm_rec k u t1)` ✓). IH with d ✓.

tm_tapp: open = tm_tapp (open_tm_rec d v t1) T; tm_ty_subst_env gives tm_tapp (θ (open ... t1)) (θ T) = tm_tapp (open_tm_rec d (θ v) (θ t1)) (θ T) by IH. RHS: open_tm_rec d (θ v) (tm_tapp (θ t1) (θ T)) = tm_tapp (open_tm_rec d (θ v) (θ t1)) (θ T) ✓.

tm_choice ✓.

The IH naming: induction t gives IHs for subterms; with `revert d` before induction, the IHs are forall d. ✓. But `auto` may not close; let me write explicit.

```coq
Lemma lc_ty_at_mono : forall k k' T, k <= k' -> lc_ty_at k T -> lc_ty_at k' T.
Proof.
  intros k k' T Hle H. revert k' Hle.
  induction H as [k i Hi | k X | k T1 T2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 | k T1 H1 IH1]; intros k' Hle; simpl in *.
  - apply lc_ty_bvar. lia.
  - apply lc_ty_fvar.
  - apply lc_ty_arrow; [apply IH1 | apply IH2]; lia.
  - apply lc_ty_all. apply IH1. lia.
```

```coq
Lemma wf_open_ty : forall Delta T U,
  wf_ty Delta (Ty_All T) -> wf_ty Delta U -> wf_ty Delta (open_ty T U).
Proof.
  intros Delta T U HAll HU.
  inversion HAll as [| | L Delta' T' Hbody]; subst.   (* need to extract L and Hbody *)
```
Inversion on wf_ty Delta (Ty_All T) gives the WF_All case: L, Delta'=Delta, T'=T, Hbody : forall X, ~ In X L -> wf_ty (X::Delta) (open_ty T (FVar X)).
Choose X := fresh_atom (L ++ Delta ++ ty_fv T). Then X ∉ L. Hbody X HX : wf_ty (X::Delta) (open_ty T (FVar X)).
Apply wf_ty_subst_env with Delta' := X::Delta, theta := fun Z => if eqb Z X then U else Ty_FVar Z.
- Need: forall Z, lc_ty_at 0 (theta Z): if Z = X then U (lc from wf_ty_lc Delta U HU ✓); else FVar Z lc ✓.
- Need: forall Z, In Z (X::Delta) -> wf_ty Delta (theta Z): Z = X → wf_ty Delta U ✓; Z ∈ Delta → theta Z = FVar Z → WF_Var ✓ (using eqb false since Z ≠ X).
- Conclusion: wf_ty Delta (ty_subst_env theta (open_ty T (FVar X))).
- Show ty_subst_env theta (open_ty T (FVar X)) = open_ty T U:
  By ty_subst_env_open_ty with theta and global lc: = open_ty_rec 0 (ty_subst_env theta (FVar X)) (ty_subst_env theta T) = open_ty_rec 0 U (ty_subst_env theta T). And ty_subst_env theta T = T since X ∉ fv T ✓ (ty_subst_env_ext: for Y ∈ fv T, Y ≠ X, theta Y = FVar Y).
  So = open_ty_rec 0 U T = open_ty T U ✓.

Now, the induction principle of has_type with the L quantifiers: has_type's constructors:
```
| T_Var : forall Delta Gamma x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> wf_ty Delta T -> has_type ...
| T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Delta Gamma T1 t2 T2, wf_ty Delta T1 -> (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type Delta (update Gamma x T1) (open_tm t2 (fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Delta Gamma (tm_abs T1 t2) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)
| T_App ...
| T_TAbs : forall (L : list atom) Delta Gamma t T, (forall X, ~ In X L -> has_type (X::Delta) Gamma (open_tm_ty t (FVar X)) (open_ty T (FVar X))) -> has_type Delta Gamma (tm_tabs t) (Ty_All T)
| T_TApp : forall Delta Gamma t T U, has_type Delta Gamma t (Ty_All T) -> wf_ty Delta U -> has_type Delta Gamma (tm_tapp t U) (open_ty T U)
| T_Choice ...
```
The induction principle: for T_Abs, the premise gives `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...` and an IH `forall x, ~ In x L -> P ...`. So the pattern might be:
`[Delta Gamma x T Hlookup Hwf | L Delta Gamma T1 t2 T2 Hwf1 Hbody IHbody | Delta Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 | L Delta Gamma t T Hbody IHbody | Delta Gamma t T U H1 IH1 HwfU | Delta Gamma t1 t2 T H1 IH1 H2 IH2]`.

Hmm, the IH for a nested forall premise: Coq's generated induction principle for constructors with premises of the form `(forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...)` gives an IH of the form `forall x, ~ In x L -> P ...`? I believe so (it recursively maps under the quantifier). Let me check by trying.

```coq
(* ---------- Monotonicity ---------- *)

Lemma lc_ty_at_mono : forall k k' T, k <= k' -> lc_ty_at k T -> lc_ty_at k' T.
Proof.
  intros k k' T Hle H. revert k' Hle.
  induction H as [k i Hi | k X | k T1 T2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 | k T1 H1 IH1];
    intros k' Hle; simpl.
  - apply lc_ty_bvar. lia.
  - apply lc_ty_fvar.
  - apply lc_ty_arrow; [apply IH1 | apply IH2]; lia.
  - apply lc_ty_all. apply IH1. lia.
Qed.
```
Hmm: in the arrow case, the goal after intros k' Hle is lc_ty_at k' (Ty_Arrow T1 T2). apply lc_ty_arrow creates lc_ty_at k' T1 and lc_ty_at k' T2; IH1 with (k' := k') and Hle; lia ✓. But `apply IH1` will need Hle : k <= k' which is in context ✓.

Wait, actually there's a subtlety: `induction H` while the goal is `forall k', k <= k' -> lc_ty_at k' T` — the induction principle must generalize k' and Hle. Since they're introduced after the revert, the goal is a forall ✓.

Term monotonicity:

```coq
Lemma lc_tm_at_mono : forall K K' k k' t,
  K <= K' -> k <= k' -> lc_tm_at K k t -> lc_tm_at K' k' t.
Proof.
  intros K K' k k' t HK Hk H. revert K' k' HK Hk.
  induction H as [K k i Hi | K k x | K k T t1 Hty Hr IH1
                 | K k t1 t2 Hr1 IH1 Hr2 IH2 | K k t1 Hr IH1
                 | K k t1 T Hr IH1 Hty | K k t1 t2 Hr1 IH1 Hr2 IH2];
    intros K' k' HK Hk; simpl.
  - apply lc_tm_bvar. lia.
  - apply lc_tm_fvar.
  - apply lc_tm_abs.
    + apply (lc_ty_at_mono K K' T); assumption.
    + apply IH1; lia.
  - apply lc_tm_app; [apply IH1; lia | apply IH2; lia].
  - apply lc_tm_tabs. apply IH1; lia.
  - apply lc_tm_tapp.
    + apply IH1; lia.
    + apply (lc_ty_at_mono K K' T); assumption.
  - apply lc_tm_choice; [apply IH1; lia | apply IH2; lia].
Qed.
```

```coq
Lemma lc_ty_open_ty_rec_close : forall k d X T,
  lc_ty_at k (open_ty_rec d (Ty_FVar X) T) -> lc_ty_at (max k (S d)) T.
Proof.
  intros k d X T. revert k d.
  induction T as [n | Y | T1 IH1 T2 IH2 | T1 IH1]; intros k d H; simpl in H.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb d n) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst n.
      apply lc_ty_bvar. apply Nat.le_trans with (S d); [lia | apply Nat.le_max_r].
```
Hmm: after `destruct (Nat.eqb d n)`, H is the lc of either FVar X or BVar n. In the true case we need to show lc_ty_at (max k (S d)) (Ty_BVar n) with n = d: need n < max k (S d), i.e., d < max k (S d) ✓ (since S d ≤ max and d < S d).
In the false case, H : lc_ty_at k (Ty_BVar n); inversion gives n < k; need n < max k (S d) ✓.
```
    + inversion H as [k0 i Hi | | |]. apply lc_ty_bvar. lia.
```
Careful with inversion naming: H : lc_ty_at k (Ty_BVar n); inversion H as [k0 i Hi | | |] — the bvar constructor has args k0 i Hi with the equation k = k0, Ty_BVar i = Ty_BVar n. Coq's inversion might not need explicit names; `inversion H; subst; apply lc_ty_bvar; lia` might work. Let me use:
```
    + inversion H. apply lc_ty_bvar. lia.
```
After inversion H, we get Hi : n < k (with k possibly renamed). lia should close.

- FVar case: H : lc_ty_at k (Ty_FVar Y) (open of FVar is itself). Goal lc_ty_at (max k (S d)) (Ty_FVar Y) → lc_ty_fvar ✓.
- Arrow case: H : lc_ty_at k (Ty_Arrow (open...) (open...)). inversion H gives H1, H2. apply lc_ty_arrow; [apply IH1 with d | apply IH2 with d]; assumption. But the max: goal is lc_ty_at (max k (S d)) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2); apply lc_ty_arrow needs components at max k (S d); IH1 k d H1 gives exactly that ✓.
- All case: H : lc_ty_at k (Ty_All (open_ty_rec (S d) (FVar X) T1)). inversion gives H1 : lc_ty_at (S k) (open_ty_rec (S d) ...). IH1 (S k) (S d) H1 : lc_ty_at (max (S k) (S (S d))) T1. Goal: lc_ty_at (max k (S d)) (Ty_All T1) → apply lc_ty_all → need lc_ty_at (S (max k (S d))) T1. Rewrite with Nat.succ_max_distr: S (max k (S d)) = max (S k) (S (S d)) ✓. So:
```
  - apply lc_ty_all. rewrite <- Nat.succ_max_distr. apply IH1. assumption.
```
Wait, the goal after lc_ty_all is lc_ty_at (S (max k (S d))) T1. We have IH1 ... : lc_ty_at (max (S k) (S (S d))) T1. `rewrite <- Nat.succ_max_distr`? Nat.succ_max_distr : S (max n m) = max (S n) (S m). To convert S (max k (S d)) to max (S k) (S (S d)), rewrite with (n := k) (m := S d) using Nat.succ_max_distr in the goal's direction: the goal has S (max k (S d)); `rewrite Nat.succ_max_distr` changes it to max (S k) (S (S d)) ✓. But careful: the rewrite might loop or apply elsewhere. Let me use `change (lc_ty_at (Nat.max (S k) (S (S d))) T1). apply IH1; assumption.` Or simply `apply (lc_ty_at_mono (max (S k) (S (S d))) (S (max k (S d))) T1)`? Hmm. Let's do:
```
    apply lc_ty_all.
    assert (Heq : S (max k (S d)) = max (S k) (S (S d))) by apply Nat.succ_max_distr.
    rewrite Heq. apply IH1. assumption.
```

```coq
Lemma lc_tm_open_ty_rec_close : forall K k d X t,
  lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_ty_rec d (Ty_FVar X) t) -> lc_tm_at (max K (S d)) k t.
Proof.
  intros K k d X t. revert K k d.
  induction t as [n | x | T t1 IH1 | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | t1 IH1 | t1 IH1 T | t1 IH1 t2 IH2];
    intros K k d H; simpl in H.
  - (* bvar: H : lc_tm_at K k (tm_bvar n) *) inversion H. apply lc_tm_bvar. assumption.
  - inversion H. apply lc_tm_fvar.
  - (* abs *) simpl in H. inversion H as [K0 k0 T0 t0 Hty Hbody | | | | | |]. 
```
Hmm, inversion with named patterns is fragile. Let me use `inversion H; subst` and then use the hypotheses. For the abs case:
H : lc_tm_at K k (tm_abs (open_ty_rec d (FVar X) T) (open_tm_ty_rec d (FVar X) t1)). Inversion gives:
- H2 : lc_ty_at K (open_ty_rec d (FVar X) T)
- H3 : lc_tm_at K (S k) (open_tm_ty_rec d (FVar X) t1)
Goal: lc_tm_at (max K (S d)) k (tm_abs T t1). apply lc_tm_abs; [apply lc_ty_open_ty_rec_close with (d:=d) in H2 | apply IH1 with (d:=d) in H3].
Note the max for the body: IH1 with K, (S k), d gives lc_tm_at (max K (S d)) (S k) t1 ✓ (matches what lc_tm_abs needs: body at S k ✓).

For `inversion H`, the hypothesis names generated are like H2, H3 etc. To be robust, use `inversion H as [K0 k0 T0 t0 Hty Hbody | K0 k0 x0 | K0 k0 T0 t0 Hty Hbody | ...]`? That's fragile. Better: `inversion H; subst; clear H.` then refer to the auto-generated names? Auto names are unpredictable. Alternative: use `inversion H as [...]` with the full constructor patterns; the names I choose bind the arguments, and for recursive premises + IH? Inversion doesn't generate IHs (it's not induction), so the premises are just hypotheses. The constructor patterns for lc_tm_at:
1. lc_tm_bvar: forall K k i, i < k -> ... → pattern [K0 k0 i Hi]
2. lc_tm_fvar: forall K k x → [K0 k0 x0]
3. lc_tm_abs: forall K k T t, lc_ty_at K T -> lc_tm_at K (S k) t -> ... → [K0 k0 T0 t0 Hty Hbody]
4. lc_tm_app: [K0 k0 t1' t2' H1 H2]
5. lc_tm_tabs: [K0 k0 t' H1]
6. lc_tm_tapp: [K0 k0 t' T' H1 H2]
7. lc_tm_choice: [K0 k0 t1' t2' H1 H2]
